My mind keeps jumping between things, like whether the new partnership at our lab might mean my position gets cut, or whether my data are wrong, and then it goes to bills piling up, my family thinking I’m falling behind, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. I know some of that might sound like normal stress, but it feels kind of constant, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries tend to come up in specific situations, like when you're around other people or during certain events, or do they seem to pop up no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s not really just one situation, I don’t think. Like, I wish I could say it’s only when something specific happens, but it kind of pops up all over the place. My mind just keeps jumping between things—like the lab partnership and whether my job could be affected, or if my data are wrong, then bills, my family thinking I’m behind, grad school stuff… it just kind of cycles. I know some of those are real things to think about, but it feels like I can’t keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with these worries, like trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my shoulders and neck are tight pretty much all day. I’ll be working on spreadsheets and realize I’m clenching my jaw without even noticing. By the evening I’ll have headaches and feel stiff, kind of like I’ve been carrying equipment around all day, even if I haven’t. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that comes on quickly, like a panic attack?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like a sudden wave where I feel terrified out of nowhere, at least. I’ve felt really wound up and uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’d call it a panic attack. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worrying and the physical symptoms like muscle tension and headaches?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about seven months, maybe. It started around when the lab’s funding shifted and the commercial partnership changed how things were being run. I kept thinking it would settle down once I adjusted, but… it’s kind of stayed constant since then. Does that sound like the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have trouble controlling or stopping the worrying, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the part I have the hardest time with. I try to tell myself, “Just focus on this one thing,” like finishing a report or checking an assay, and I’ll mean it. But then one worry kind of triggers another, and pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen while my head is running through every possible way things could go wrong. I can sometimes pull myself back for a minute, but it doesn’t really stay. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I wouldn’t say hopeless, exactly. I still do things, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Maybe I’ve been less enthusiastic about stuff sometimes, but I figured that’s just because I’m tired or preoccupied. I can still enjoy things once I’m there, I think. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Is that an okay distinction?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy don’t feel as rewarding or interesting to you anymore, even when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that “it feels empty even while I’m doing it” kind of way. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting more irritable or easily annoyed lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m snapping at people or anything like that. Maybe I get a little short in my head over small stuff, but I try not to show it. I don’t think it’s been a big problem, at least not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean by irritable?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy almost every day, even if you haven’t done anything physically draining?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have noticed that. Is that the kind of tired you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Is that what you mean by irritable?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy almost every day, even if you haven’t done anything physically draining?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have noticed that. Is that the kind of tired you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, like reading through a report or following along in a meeting, even when you're trying your best to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I know some of that might sound like normal stress, but it feels kind of constant, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries tend to come up in specific situations, like when you're around other people or during certain events, or do they seem to pop up no matter what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s not really just one situation, I don’t think. My mind just keeps jumping between things—like the lab partnership and whether my job could be affected, or if my data are wrong, then bills, my family thinking I’m behind, grad school stuff… it just kind of cycles. I know some of those are real things to think about, but it feels like I can’t keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with these worries, like trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my shoulders and neck are tight pretty much all day. I know some of those are real things to think about, but it feels like I can’t keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with these worries, like trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my shoulders and neck are tight pretty much all day. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that comes on quickly, like a panic attack?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worrying and the physical symptoms like muscle tension and headaches?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about seven months, maybe. It started around when the lab’s funding shifted and the commercial partnership changed how things were being run. I kept thinking it would settle down once I adjusted, but… it’s kind of stayed constant since then. Does that sound like the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have trouble controlling or stopping the worrying, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the part I have the hardest time with. I try to tell myself, “Just focus on this one thing,” like finishing a report or checking an assay, and I’ll mean it. But then one worry kind of triggers another, and pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen while my head is running through every possible way things could go wrong. I can sometimes pull myself back for a minute, but it doesn’t really stay. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I wouldn’t say hopeless, exactly. I can still enjoy things once I’m there, I think. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or where you used to enjoy something but now it just feels like a chore or doesn’t feel good anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like that, not really. Is that an okay distinction?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy don’t feel as rewarding or interesting to you anymore, even when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that “it feels empty even while I’m doing it” kind of way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to look forward to, like hobbies or time with friends, feel more like obligations or don’t bring you the same satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, sometimes things feel like more effort than they probably should, but I still can enjoy seeing people or doing things once I actually get myself there. I haven’t really noticed that I don’t care about them anymore or anything. Maybe I’m not explaining that well—does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you experience a lot of anxiety or worry about various things almost every day, rather than just occasionally or during particularly stressful times?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, yes. I keep trying to tell myself it’s just normal stress, but it feels like my mind is kind of constantly hopping from one thing to the next. Like whether the lab’s new partnership means my position might get cut, or whether the data I turned in are wrong, or bills piling up, or if my family thinks I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school, even if there’s not one clear reason to think that right then. It’s… a lot of different things, almost every day. I’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s kind of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing bad was happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not like sad most of the day for several days in a row. I mean, I have off days where I feel kind of worn down or discouraged, but it doesn’t feel like this heavy sadness that just sits there all day. At least, I haven’t noticed it that way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, like reading a report or following a conversation, even when you're trying your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I have moments where I reread the same paragraph or realize I wasn’t fully listening, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. I can usually get through things if I make myself slow down or double-check. \n\nSo… maybe a little, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a clear reason for it at the moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always have a clear “reason” in the moment, but my mind just kind of jumps around anyway.\n\nLike I came in because it keeps going from, is our lab’s new partnership going to mean my position gets cut, to what if the data I turned in are wrong, to bills piling up, to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind… and then somehow I’m worrying I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nI know some of that is normal to think about, but it feels like it’s most days, and it’s hard to shut off."
